## Plan de tests à ajouter

Consolidation de tous les `proposed_test`, regroupés par faisabilité. Chaque test verrouille la non-régression du finding lié.

### headless-unit (127)

- [x] [`dispatcher-boundfunc-ptr-drops-subscribers`](#f-dispatcher-boundfunc-ptr-drops-subscribers) — headless-unit in tests/: load hook_dispatcher.ahk, define two trivial functions A,B, register A.Bind() and B.Bind() (distinct objects) for EVT_KB_CHAR, then AssertEqual(HookDispatcher._subscribers[EVT_KB_CHAR].Length, 2). Then register A's SAME bound reference twice and assert Length stays at its prior value (dedup still works). Fails before the fix (count is 1), passes after.
- [x] [`sensors-bypass-pause-suspend`](#f-sensors-bypass-pause-suspend) — meta-static: a tests/meta/ test that reads each keylogger_*.ahk sensor file and asserts the tick/handler function bodies contain an `A_IsSuspended` guard (mirror of test_require_state_pattern.ahk's source-text scan). headless-unit: load keylogger.ahk's KL_AppendLog logic under a stub where Keylogger.initialized=true and A_IsSuspended is forced true via a seam, push an entry, and AssertEqual that Keylogger._pending_entries.Length stayed 0 (fails before the chokepoint guard, passes after).
- [x] [`dispatcher-unregister-never-removes`](#f-dispatcher-unregister-never-removes) — headless-unit: register A.Bind() stored in a variable for an event, assert Length 1, call Unregister with the SAME stored reference, assert Length 0. Add a meta/static test asserting keyboard_hook.ahk does not call `.Bind()` inside both KHStart and KHStop on the same symbol (grep: Register and Unregister must reference a stored global, not an inline .Bind()).
- [x] [`textpresskey-drops-string-modifiers`](#f-textpresskey-drops-string-modifiers) — headless-unit: stub _AHK_SendInput to capture payloads, call TextPressKey("Tab","Shift") and AssertEqual captured to "+{Tab}"; TextPressKey("Tab","Ctrl Shift") -> "^+{Tab}"; TextPressKey("BackSpace","Blind") -> a payload containing "Blind". Fails today (bare {Tab}/{BackSpace}), passes after.
- [x] [`uia-selection-query-on-hot-path`](#f-uia-selection-query-on-hot-path) — Headless-unit: refactor the selection probe behind an injectable seam (like _SendHook) — a test sets a fake selection-provider that records how many times it was invoked and can simulate a slow/throwing provider. Assert _OnPrefixChar does NOT invoke the provider when wrap_text_if_selected is off, and that a throwing/timed-out provider is caught and yields the no-wrap fall-through (char still delivered) rather than aborting the callback. ---

## Vérifications écartées (false-positives)

- **Personal-editor saves spawn python synchronously (RunWait) while the user may be typing** (hotstrings-config, medium) — I read the cited code. The three RunWait sites are real and exactly as described: personal_toml_editor.ahk:345 (WritePersonalToml), :433 (WritePersonalInfoToml), and the twin in toml_helpers.ahk:365 (TOML_FormatViaScript). They are reached from user-clicked GUI button handlers (_AddEntry/_SaveEntry/_DeleteEntry/_NewSection/_RenameSection/_DeleteSection -> _SaveData/direct -> Write*Toml). The editor is a plain non-modal Gui() (Gui_Create in ui_style.ahk:307-311). No Critical anywhere in the path. So the *shape* of the finding is accurate.

But the finding's core mechanism — "RunWait delays the low-level keyboard hook callbacks / InputHook OnChar dispatch, so keystrokes are dropped/sticky" — is wrong, and the project's own learned invariant proves it. PROJECT_MEMORY.md:1144 (project_updater_nonblocking_http) is explicit about WHY input freezes: a *synchronous WinHttp Req.Send()* is a native COM call that does NOT pump messages, so the single AHK pseudo-thread is stuck in native code and no other thread (hotkey/timer/InputHook callback) can be dispatched. RunWait is categorically different: AHK v2's RunWait pumps the message queue and keeps dispatching pseudo-threads while the child process runs — hotkeys, SetTimers, and the message-driven InputHook OnChar/OnKeyDown/OnKeyUp callbacks continue to fire for its whole duration. The single HookDispatcher InputHook("V L0") and the remapping Send()s therefore are NOT starved; keystrokes are not dropped. The catastrophic LowLevelHooksTimeout drop the finding invokes requires the hook callback itself to stall, which RunWait does not cause. Moreover the same memo (line 1150) explicitly blesses synchronous external work on USER-INITIATED paths (the user clicked Save and is actively waiting) — precisely this case — and reserves the async requirement for unprompted background polls. So even taken at face value this path is the project's accepted pattern, not the forbidden one.

The dispositive fact, though, is that the RunWait is currently DEAD CODE. Every call site computes FormatScript := ScriptDir . "tools\\format_toml.py", but git shows commit 9b23638f9 ("chore: clean files") renamed the script tools/{ => dev}/format_toml.py without updating the AHK references (git grep confirms all three .ahk sites still say tools\\format_toml.py; the file now lives only at tools\\dev\\format_toml.py). Therefore FileExist(FormatScript) is False at runtime and RunWait never executes. There is no live input-lag impact at all on the current codebase. The finding describes a latent stall behind a guard that is currently never satisfied, with an incorrect severity rationale.
- **A_MaxHotkeysPerInterval throttle can abort a hold thread mid-Down, leaving a modifier or the nav layer permanently latched** (tap-holds, high) — I read all cited code (modules/tap_holds/{nav_layer,lalt,win,capslock,enter,space}.ahk, lib/nav_layer_helpers.ahk, ErgoptiPlus.ahk:103/111, adapters/text_sender.ahk). The finding's central causal chain is mechanically impossible under the AHK v2 concurrency model.

(1) WRONG SEMANTICS OF A_MaxHotkeysPerInterval. This setting throttles the LAUNCH of a NEW hotkey pseudo-thread within A_HotkeyInterval; it does NOT abort, interrupt, or unwind a thread that is ALREADY RUNNING. The hold pattern's thread is exactly that: it has already fired, executed `TextPressKey(ModKey,"Down")`, and is now parked inside `KeyWait(...)` (lalt.ahk:263-271, capslock.ahk:142-152, enter.ahk:64-66, win.ahk:57-60). It was already counted at launch. Exceeding the ceiling afterwards cannot reach into that running KeyWait and skip the paired `TextPressKey(ModKey,"Up")`. When the ceiling is actually hit, AHK puts up a modal "attempting to run too many hotkeys" dialog and the user continues or the WHOLE script exits — there is no mode where one in-flight thread is silently killed mid-Down while the rest keep running. So "the thread being throttled skips the Up, LShift stays Down" describes a behavior AHK does not have.

(2) INTERRUPTION DOES NOT DISCARD THE CLEANUP. AHK is cooperative: when a higher-priority thread (e.g. nav_layer.ahk:42-44 `SC03A:: { DisableLayer() LAltCapsLockShortcut() }`) interrupts a hold thread parked in KeyWait, AHK SUSPENDS and later RESUMES the interrupted thread — it never drops it. The hold thread resumes its KeyWait and still reaches its post-wait `Up`/`DisableLayer()`. There is no static-readable path where interruption causes the parked thread to be abandoned before its next line.

(3) NO AUTO-REPEAT RE-ENTRY KILL. Every hold-layer/hold-modifier hotkey uses `$`/`*$` and relies on the default #MaxThreadsPerHotkey 1 (grep confirms it is NEVER raised; A_HotkeyInterval is left at default; space.ahk:163 documents the reliance). Auto-repeat of the held key therefore cannot spawn a second competing thread.

(4) CROSS-HANDLER DisableLayer IS BENIGN. DisableLayer() (nav_layer_helpers.ahk:37-41) is idempotent: LayerEnabled:=False plus ceiling:=150. If nav_layer's SC03A runs it while a layer-hold thread is parked, that thread's own later DisableLayer() merely re-sets False. No latch results — and crucially the cited handler is a deliberate gated feature (LAlt+CapsLock on the layer), not an accidental race.

What is REAL but minor: A_MaxHotkeysPerInterval is a shared global mutated without save/restore (boot=150 in ErgoptiPlus.ahk:103, Wheel handlers=1000 in nav_layer.ahk:67/71, DisableLayer resets 150). That is sloppy, but it only changes how often the warning DIALOG appears; it is a UX-tuning threshold, never a correctness primitive, and cannot produce a stuck modifier. The proposed try/finally also guards a non-existent failure mode: KeyWait does not throw, so there is no exception to protect the Up against. The stuck-modifier family IS real elsewhere here (the documented Kana SC138 prefix latch across Suspend), but THIS specific mechanism is not it.
- **RAlt is bound by both the AltGr tap-hold and the nav layer; the LayerEnabled gate plus prefix latch can drop AltGr characters or fire Escape** (tap-holds, medium) — I read all cited code: modules/tap_holds/altgr.ahk (RAlt:: and SC01D & ~SC138:: under `#HotIf not LayerEnabled and not IsOnboardingActive() and TapHoldIsConfigured(...)`), nav_layer.ahk:75-79 (RAlt:: and SC01D & ~SC138:: under `#HotIf LayerEnabled`, firing ActionLayer("{Escape}")), lib/nav_layer_helpers.ahk, and modules/tap_holds/space.ahk (SpaceTapHoldLayer).

The finding is a false positive on three independent grounds.

(1) It relabels an INTENDED FEATURE as a lost keystroke. When the user holds Space, SpaceTapHoldLayer() sets `LayerEnabled := True` then PARKS in `KeyWait("SC039","U")` for the whole hold. While the nav layer is active, RAlt -> Escape is the designed remap (it sits among the full nav-layer keymap: arrows, Home/End, etc.). The user deliberately entered the layer; getting Escape rather than an accented char is exactly the contract.

(2) The scenario is self-contradictory. "Hold Space (=nav) AND simultaneously press AltGr+e to type é" cannot be a natural prose combo: while Space is held you ARE in the nav layer and are not typing letters. Producing é requires releasing Space first to return to the base layer, at which point `LayerEnabled` is False and altgr.ahk owns RAlt. There is no realistic typing pattern where the user holds the layer-activation key while expecting base-layer AltGr output.

(3) The core "#HotIf down/up split race" mechanism does not exist in THIS code. The nav-layer RAlt:: is a self-contained handler — it calls ActionLayer("{Escape}") and returns; it has NO up-phase, NO `RAlt Up::` partner, and does NOT KeyWait on RAlt. So there is no "key-down handler awaiting a key-up that lands in a different #HotIf context" to strand. The altgr.ahk handler performs its tap/hold decision via an INTERNAL `KeyWait("RAlt", ...)` inside one thread (not split across two hotkey contexts), and its `RAlt Up::` partner merely does UpdateLastSentCharacter("") — a trivial state clear that can lose nothing. Moreover `LayerEnabled` is STABLE during the window: it flips only on Space press/release, so each RAlt event reads a steady value; there is no concurrent writer producing a torn read in the cited scenario.

The SC138 kana-latch worry is misattributed: the custom combo here latches SC01D (LCtrl) as prefix, not SC138, and the genuine SC138 kana-strand is already handled by separate drains (ToggleSuspend KeyWait at ErgoptiPlus.ahk:1996, ResetScriptComboKeys at 1671). The finding shows no concrete latch path through the nav/altgr dual binding. Two `RAlt::` in two files under mutually exclusive #HotIf is the canonical AHK variant-hotkey pattern, not a binding conflict. The proposed "single-owner / capture snapshot" fix would add complexity and risk breaking the documented nav-layer RAlt->Escape behavior for zero real defect.
- **nav_layer SC038::TextPressKey('LAlt','Up') and backspace+layer release can leave LAlt or the layer latched on quick release** (tap-holds, medium) — I read all cited code. The finding bundles three sub-claims; the load-bearing one (its root_cause) is refuted, and the other two are either admitted-harmless or misdescribed.

CLAIM A — "LAltCapsLockShortcut() indexes Features['shortcuts']['lalt_caps_lock'][...] which could be unset and throws (swallowed in a hotkey thread)". FALSE.
- base_modifier.ahk:41-66: LAltCapsLockShortcut() is a pure if/else-if cascade over the 10 keys with NO trailing else. If all are false it falls through to a clean no-op; it only throws if a key is genuinely ABSENT from the Map.
- The keys cannot be absent: Features is built once by ManifestBuildFeaturesMap() (ErgoptiPlus.ahk:704) from _generated/features_manifest.ahk, which defines all 10 lalt_caps_lock entries (manifest lines 212-221) with explicit defaults. The builder (manifest_reader.ahk:186-223) inserts every manifest entry; no code path deletes these keys.
- Internal contradiction: the SC03A nav-layer handler's own #HotIf gate (nav_layer.ahk:29-40) reads 7 of those same keys to decide whether to arm. If they could be unset, the #HotIf would throw during context evaluation and the body would never run — so by the time LAltCapsLockShortcut() executes, the keys provably exist.
- Already tested: test_shortcuts.ahk:256-266 (TestShortcuts_LaltCapsLock_NoActionNoSend) calls LAltCapsLockShortcut() with all 10 slots false and asserts a clean no-op; test_shortcuts.ahk:235-254 asserts each send-based action does not throw. The finding's proposed test ("stub Features WITHOUT the keys, assert no uncaught throw") exercises a state the manifest builder cannot produce.

CLAIM B — "double DisableLayer()". REAL but admitted-harmless by the finding itself. Confirmed: DisableLayer() (nav_layer_helpers.ahk:37-41) just sets LayerEnabled:=False + LED, fully idempotent; covered by test_nav_layer_helpers.ahk:40-46.

CLAIM C — "fire-and-forget {LAlt Up} at nav_layer.ahk:52 can desync OS LAlt". Misdescribed mechanism, no real harm given the AHK model:
- nav_layer.ahk:52 (SC038::TextPressKey("LAlt","Up")) is the SC038 AUTO-REPEAT suppressor (comment: prevents VSCode multicursor), not part of the CapsLock teardown the scenario narrates. The scenario's CapsLock path is nav_layer.ahk:42 (SC03A::DisableLayer() LAltCapsLockShortcut()) which emits NO {LAlt Up} at all. The finding conflates the SC03A and SC038 paths into one impossible sequence.
- In the backspace+layer config, block 4.5 (lalt.ahk:197-215) claims SC038 via *SC038:: (no ~, so the physical LAlt is CONSUMED, never reaching the OS) and activates the layer. Once LayerEnabled flips true, block 4.5's #HotIf (...and not LayerEnabled) goes false and nav_layer.ahk:52 takes over for SC038 repeats. Because the OS never saw an LAlt-down (consumed), emitting {LAlt Up} is a no-op against the driver's own key.
- The "real Alt held" desync needs an independent Alt, but LAlt (SC038) IS the layer key here — there is exactly one LAlt and it is consumed by the driver. No second Alt exists to desync.

AHK concurrency check: the double-teardown the finding worries about (SC03A thread calling DisableLayer() while block 4.5 sits in KeyWait("SC038","U") at lalt.ahk:213) is a legitimate cooperative interruption, but both teardown calls are idempotent no-throw operations, so the interleave is benign. No torn state, no swallowed throw, no lost keystroke.

Residual: the only defensible point is style — nav_layer.ahk:43 crams two statements on one physical line ("DisableLayer() LAltCapsLockShortcut()"). Both still execute; it is a readability smell, not a correctness or suspend-pause bug. The finding's category (suspend-pause-leak) and medium severity are unwarranted.
- **Space hold-modifier path blocks the keyboard thread in InputHook('L1 T3') for up to 3s before the modifier is applied** (tap-holds, medium) — I read the cited code (modules/tap_holds/space.ahk:44-160) and the sibling InputHook user (one_shot_shift.ahk) and the Critical-serialization meta-test.

The finding's CORE claim — that InputHook.Wait() "blocks the keyboard thread" such that a keystroke is "silently DROPPED" (LowLevelHooksTimeout) — is FALSE under the AHK model. InputHook.Wait is the AHK-blessed way to COLLECT input; while it blocks the *hotkey handler* from returning, it actively pumps messages and the InputHook itself is capturing. It does NOT hold Critical across a Sleep and does NOT starve the low-level keyboard hook the way a synchronous WinHTTP/ClipWait/sqlite call inside a hook callback would. LowLevelHooksTimeout drops happen when a hook callback does blocking work and never yields back to the OS hook chain; InputHook.Wait yields normally and keeps the hook live. So the user's #1 concern (a silently dropped keystroke on the hot path) does not occur here. That invalidates the "medium / hot-path-blocking" framing.

The L1-capture-then-re-emit is the DOCUMENTED, DELIBERATE design (space.ahk:24-42): "InputHook L1 captures the next key without any modifier active, so Space auto-repeat cannot produce Shift+Space or Ctrl+Space." It is the mechanism that PREVENTS a bug (held-Space auto-repeat firing Ctrl+Space), not an accident.

The finding's flagship scenario is factually wrong about the code. It claims "_SpaceHoldCtrl sends {LCtrl Down}^a — but the user has already pressed 'c' ... Ctrl released, dropping Ctrl+c." But _SpaceHoldCtrl (lines 118-126) does NOT release Ctrl after ^a: it does KeyWait("SC039","U T2"), holding {LCtrl Down} until Space is physically released. So a 'c' pressed while Space is still held lands as Ctrl+c — the chorded shortcut the finding says breaks actually works. The InputHook is created fresh, waits for exactly one char, and is finished before any SendInput, so there is no synthetic-re-entrancy / double-processing of ^a either.

What survives is small and NOT what the finding is rated on: InputHook("L1 T3") hardcodes the literal 3 (seconds). That is a magic-number violation of project rule 5.1 and an inconsistency with one_shot_shift.ahk, which correctly reads the named ONE_SHOT_SHIFT_TIMEOUT_SEC from the timings registry (constants.ahk:48-59). A 3s ceiling means: if the user holds Space past threshold and then pauses, the modifier stays "pending" (handler stuck in Wait) until a key arrives or 3s elapse — a minor latency/consistency wart, not a dropped key. Whether fast physical typing can reorder against the post-Wait SendInput is not statically decidable and would need live timing.

Net: the bug as described (blocking hot path → lost/reordered keystroke, chorded Ctrl+c dropped) is a false positive against the actual code and the AHK concurrency model. The only legitimate residue is a low-severity magic-number/config-consistency cleanup.
- **GestureDispatch and its blocking actions are not gated by A_IsSuspended** (gestures, medium) — I enumerated every caller of GESTURE_ACTIONS[name].Fn() / .Fn.Call() in the codebase (grep): gestures.ahk:1702 (inside GestureDispatch), ErgoptiPlus.ahk:1691 (RunScriptShortcutAction), ErgoptiPlus.ahk:1751 (RunKeyboardShortcutAction), tap_holds/constants.ahk:80 (_TapHoldFireAction), and tests/test_gestures.ahk:120 (test only). I then traced the ENTRY POINT of each alternate caller — and the finding's central premise (that these are "non-hotkey paths" reached via timer/tap-hold runtime/menu that bypass Suspend) is factually false:

1. Tap-hold runtime (_TapHoldFireAction): every dispatch (_TabDispatch, _DeleteDispatch, _LAltDispatch, _CapsLockDispatch, etc.) is invoked ONLY from #HotIf static hotkeys — e.g. tab.ahk:111/133 ($SC00F::, SC00F::), delete.ahk:101 ($SC053::), lalt.ahk:309 (SC038::). These are ordinary hotkeys. The InputHook usages in the tap-hold modules (one_shot_shift.ahk:27, space.ahk:51, enter.ahk) are for the HOLD/modifier phase capturing the next key — they never call _TapHoldFireAction. No SetTimer/OnMessage fires a tap action.
2. Keyboard shortcuts (RunKeyboardShortcutAction): registered via Hotkey(_KbSend, ...) at ErgoptiPlus.ahk:989 — an ordinary hotkey.
3. Script shortcuts (RunScriptShortcutAction): reached via _ScriptAltGr*Handler, all registered via Hotkey(...) at ErgoptiPlus.ahk:2144-2171 — ordinary hotkeys.
4. The "tap picker" referenced by the comment at gestures.ahk:525-527 is ShowActionPicker (ErgoptiPlus.ahk:1861, tray_menu.ahk:2321) — a config GUI that only LISTS actions and writes the chosen one to config (SetKeyboardShortcutAction / _TH_ApplyTap). It never calls .Fn(). The comment literally says "exposed here so the tap picker can list them."

Pause in this project IS native Suspend: ToggleSuspend (ErgoptiPlus.ahk:1994) calls Suspend(-1) and nothing else for arming. Per the AHK model and the project invariant, Suspend auto-disarms ALL ordinary hotkeys/hotstrings. Since EVERY path to a gesture action is an ordinary hotkey, all of them are disarmed during pause exactly like the ^#+F1 touchpad hotkeys. The genuinely Suspend-bypassing surfaces (InputHook fan-out) are already explicitly gated by HookDispatcher.Dispatch's `if A_IsSuspended\n return` (hook_dispatcher.ahk:188-192), and none of those subscribers call a gesture Fn.

Therefore the scenario "a tap-hold fires a gesture action while paused" cannot occur: the tap-hold hotkey that would call _TapHoldFireAction is itself disarmed by Suspend. Adding an A_IsSuspended guard to GestureDispatch is harmless but redundant, and would NOT even cover the alternate callers anyway since they bypass GestureDispatch entirely — so the proposed fix does not match its own stated threat model. The invariant "pause silences ALL features" is NOT violated here.
- **Walker calls in the ingest loop are wrapped in a bare try with no logging — silent aggregation failures** (keylogger-core, medium) — I read all three cited sites plus the surrounding ingest/walker code. The finding splits into two claims that must be judged separately.

CLAIM A — "a malformed typing entry makes KLW_WalkTypingEntry throw partway through, leaving a partial aggregation, swallowed by the bare try" — is a FALSE POSITIVE for two independent reasons:

1. The entry-shape gate. Entries reaching the dispatch loop are produced exclusively by KL_ReadNewTodayLog (line 1194-1196) and KL_FlushBuffer's in-RAM queue. The disk path only pushes an entry when `entry is Map && entry.Has("type")` (line 1195); a JSONL line that fails to parse is caught and skipped at line 1197-1199. So by line 1233 every `entry` is GUARANTEED to be a Map carrying a "type" key. That means line 1240 `t := entry["type"]` (inside the try) cannot throw, and — more tellingly — line 1234 `KL_BuildInserts(entry)` which does `t := entry["type"]` UNGUARDED at line 1066 and is OUTSIDE the try also cannot throw. The finding's premise of a non-Map / type-less entry reaching the walker is unreachable.

2. The walker is exhaustively defensive against exactly the shapes the finding proposes. KLW_WalkTypingEntry guards `if !(entry is Map) return` (375-376), `if !(events is Array) return` (381-382), and per element `if !(ev is Array) || ev.Length < 2 continue` (473-474). Every field read goes through KLW_GetMap (255-259), which returns a default on a missing/non-Map key. The only raw bracket indexes inside the loop are ev[1]/ev[2] (already gated by line 473) and accesses on walker-built internal structures (entry_marks, bs_entry, batch rows, ctx["recent_typing"] popped under an `is Map` guard at 557/562). There is no realistic throw path for a structurally-valid typing entry. The finding's own proposed regression test — "call KLW_WalkTypingEntry with events containing a non-Array element and assert it does not throw / ctx unchanged" — would PASS on the current code with no fix, because line 474 `continue`s past it and the ctx writeback is deferred to lines 829-835 (the finding concedes this). A test that passes without the change is not a regression test for a real bug.

The concurrency dimension is also a non-issue: KL_IngestOnce is a SetTimer callback (1555-1557), KLW.batch is filled only on this single ingest pseudo-thread and drained by KLR_InjectKlwBatch, also SetTimer-driven; AHK's cooperative single-threading serializes both, and the integer `+=` accumulations are not torn by any interleaving that can occur here.

CLAIM B — "lines 1239-1250 are a bare `try {}` with no catch and no logger, violating rule 5.3 (no silent failures)" — is literally TRUE as written. If the walker ever threw for a reason OTHER than the (unreachable) malformed-input scenario — e.g. a genuine future bug in the walker's own logic, or a new event schema mishandled — the error would vanish with zero breadcrumb, and because the enclosing tick is a SetTimer callback the throw is swallowed by AHK anyway. So the missing catch+log is a real hygiene gap. But the residual risk is "no log line if a future walker bug throws," not the "silent aggregation degradation from malformed data" that the finding's medium severity and impact rest on. That data-integrity story does not materialize, so medium is too high; this is a low/info code-hygiene improvement.
- **Scroll-burst accumulator can be torn between the wheel handler and the flush timer** (keylogger-sensors, high) — I read the actual code. KL_Mouse_FlushScroll (keylogger_mouse.ahk:319-352): the read of the accumulator (ticks/h_ticks/start, lines 324-326) and its reset to 0 (lines 328-331) form a CONTIGUOUS block of plain local reads and static-field assignments with NO yielding call between them. The one yielding call, MF_ShouldFilter() (line 323, which yields via WinGetID/WinGetProcessName/WinGetTitle/WinGetClass in MF_RefreshFocus), runs BEFORE the snapshot; the IO (KL_AppendLog, line 342) runs AFTER the reset. KL_Mouse_AccumScroll (lines 289-303) is symmetric: its mutation (scroll_start/scroll_ticks+=delta/scroll_last, lines 296-299) is yield-free; the only calls that can yield — KL_BumpMouseScroll() (a lone increment, keylogger.ahk:1633, no yield anyway) and SetTimer (line 302) — run AFTER the mutation is already complete.

The finding's central claim is that a tick is lost "between the flush's read of ticks and its zeroing of scroll_ticks." That window does not exist as a thread-switch point. AHK v2 is cooperatively multi-threaded: a pseudo-thread (here the scroll-flush SetTimer callback, or a HookDispatcher wheel subscriber) is interrupted ONLY at a genuine rest point — during Sleep, during a blocking built-in, or when the thread ends — never preemptively between two ordinary non-blocking assignment lines. The finding mis-states the model ("a thread can be interrupted at a rest point (between lines...)"): "between lines" is a candidate boundary only when the surrounding statements yield; AHK does not time-slice arbitrary line boundaries. KLMouse is a plain static-field class (lines 94-142) with no __Get/__Set, so even the field accesses cannot yield.

Direction A (wheel handler preempts the flush): impossible — lines 324-331 have no rest point, so a queued wheel-dispatch thread cannot run until the flush returns to the message pump, by which time the snapshot+reset is already atomic. Direction B (flush timer preempts the wheel handler): impossible — a SetTimer fires only at a rest point, and AccumScroll's mutation block has none; the mutation completes before KL_BumpMouseScroll/SetTimer.

HookDispatcher.Dispatch (hook_dispatcher.ahk:187-198) does NOT hold Critical (the Critical calls are in Register/Unregister, mutating subscriber arrays), so the wheel handler runs at normal interruptibility — but that does not matter, because interruptibility only manifests at rest points, of which the accumulator's RMW blocks have none.

This is corroborated by the project's own serialization model in tests/meta/test_input_serialization.ahk: the maintainer added Critical only to regions that contain a SendEvent/Sleep (a real rest point) — _RemapEmit, _OnPrefixChar, HSE_DispatchMatch — precisely because that is where AHK can interleave. The scroll accumulator has no Send/Sleep in its critical region, so the documented failure mode (interleave at a Send/Sleep) cannot arise here.

Edge note: the comment at line 327 about "reset before IO so rapid re-entry doesn't double-log" is correctly placed and the only real concern (double-logging across the KL_AppendLog yield) is already handled by zeroing before the IO. After the flush zeroes the counter, a subsequent wheel tick legitimately seeds a NEW burst (scroll_start back to its tick) — that is the intended burst-boundary behavior, not a lost tick.
- **app_categories Map mutated by the flush path while the deferred-save timer enumerates it** (keylogger-sensors, high) — The finding's central, load-bearing claim is that "AHK v2 raises 'object was modified during enumeration' inside the timer callback" when KL_AppCat_Get inserts a key into KLAppCat.categories while KL_AppCat_Save enumerates it (keylogger_app_categories.ahk:227-228). I read the actual code and then empirically tested the claim against the installed AutoHotkey v2.0 (C:\Program Files\AutoHotkey\v2\AutoHotkey64.exe). The claim is categorically false.

EMPIRICAL TEST 1 (Map mutation during enumeration): inserting a brand-new key mid-`for k,v in map` does NOT throw (loop visited 4 of an original 3 + 1 inserted), inserting then checking Count does NOT throw, and deleting mid-enumeration does NOT throw. AHK v2 has no "modified during enumeration" exception — that is a .NET/Java semantic the finding incorrectly imported. So the described chain (enum throws -> error swallowed in SetTimer -> FileDelete already ran -> file left deleted/empty) cannot occur via that mechanism.

EMPIRICAL TEST 2 (atomicity): the production save loop body is a single pure assignment `obj[k] := v` (line 228) with NO Sleep, FileRead, FileAppend, or any blocking built-in inside the loop. The yielding I/O (FileDelete line 229, FileAppend line 230) runs strictly AFTER the loop completes, and operates on the private copy `obj`, never on KLAppCat.categories. A tight pure-assignment `for` loop is a CPU-bound region with no rest point: I confirmed a 5000-entry pure-assignment enumeration ran fully to completion before a pending -1ms timer could fire (interrupted_mid_loop=NO). AHK v2 is cooperatively single-threaded; a timer/InputHook thread can only preempt at a rest point (Sleep/blocking built-in/between lines while interruptible), and this loop offers none until it ends. The real categories Map is ~80 DEFAULTS plus a handful of learned apps, so the loop finishes in microseconds.

EMPIRICAL TEST 3 (stress): 3000 enumeration passes with a 1ms periodic mutator firing concurrently (3381 inserts landed) produced throws=0. A handful (9/3000 ~0.3%) of passes saw a Count that differed from the pass-start snapshot, i.e. enumeration can occasionally race an insert that lands at the boundary — but the outcome is a benign off-by-one in a throwaway copy (the first-seen app is simply captured in this save or the next deferred save). Never an exception, never corruption, never a deleted-but-not-rewritten file from enumeration.

The only way app_categories.json is left deleted is if FileAppend itself throws (disk full / file locked) between lines 229-230 — that is unrelated to the enumeration race the finding describes, and it self-heals: KL_AppCat_Reload (lines 201-221) always rebuilds from KLAppCatConst.DEFAULTS first and re-merges, then re-saves, so a missing file is reconstructed with all built-ins plus in-memory learned apps on the next reload/init. The project's own meta-test (tests/meta/test_input_serialization.ahk) already documents that the keystroke hot path needs explicit Critical only where Sends/Sleeps create rest points — reinforcing that a no-yield enumeration does not.

Conclusion: the high-severity "enumeration throws -> swallowed -> data loss/corruption" mechanism does not exist in AHK v2. The proposed Critical/Clone snapshot fix guards against a bug that cannot fire.
- **GetActiveApp writes the freshness timestamp BEFORE the snapshot fields — a re-entrant call mid-update reads a stale-but-valid snapshot** (config-toml, medium) — I read the actual code. active_app_cache.ahk:91-95 does write `_ActiveAppCache.ts := Now` FIRST, then Class/Exe/IsNotepad/IsMicrosoftOffice — so the finding's description of the field order is factually correct. But the torn-read MECHANISM it claims cannot fire under the AHK v2 cooperative model, for two independent reasons.

(1) No rest point in the dangerous window. Lines 91-95 are five plain property stores on a plain object literal (the cache is `{...}` at lines 57-63, no class, so no `__Set` meta-function runs). There is no Sleep, no blocking built-in, no message-pump call, and no user-code callback between line 91 and line 95. AHK v2 launches an interrupting pseudo-thread only when it polls the OS message queue — which happens during Sleep, blocking built-ins (WinGet*, ClipWait, RunWait…), and similar yield points, NOT between two adjacent plain assignments. The only blocking built-ins here are WinGetClass/WinGetProcessName at lines 88-89, which execute BEFORE `ts` is published. An interrupt landing there makes a reader see the OLD ts: if it is expired the reader does its own full refresh; if it is still within TTL the reader returns the fully-consistent PREVIOUS snapshot. Either way, no torn mix. The finding's own justification ("the WinGet* calls are rest points, so an interrupt is realistic right around the refresh") actually argues against itself — the rest points are upstream of the ts publish, and the 91→95 gap it needs has none.

(2) The exact scenario it paints is Critical-protected. The "user types and triggers a hotstring" path is _OnPrefixChar (hotstring_prefix_watcher.ahk), which sets `Critical("On")` at line 723 BEFORE HSE_FeedChar (735) → HSE_DispatchMatch (749) → GetActiveApp() (engine_main.ahk:1293 and engine.ahk:505/327). Under Critical the thread is uninterruptible and incoming hotkey/timer messages are buffered, so on the primary fire path GetActiveApp can neither be interrupted as the writer nor fire as an interrupting reader. The scenario's "a SetTimer/hook fires before the flags update and itself calls GetActiveApp" is impossible on that path.

I also confirmed the only writer of the cache fields is GetActiveApp itself (InvalidateActiveAppCache touches only ts); there is no WinEvent hook or high-frequency timer mutating it (grep of _ActiveAppCache shows only the engine call sites). The remaining non-Critical readers (#HotIf MicrosoftApps() in layout.ahk:759, gesture/ctrl shortcuts, and the Space tap-hold path _SpaceTap → HSE_DispatchMatch which reaches GetActiveApp at 1293 before its own Critical at 1341) are all driven by discrete user keystrokes — low frequency relative to a five-instruction store burst with no yield point — and still hit reason (1): there is no interruptible boundary inside 91→95 for them to wedge into.

The project uses AHK v2 defaults (no Thread "Interrupt"/A_UninterruptibleTime/#MaxThreads overrides anywhere), which further protects fresh threads from interruption for the first ~15 ms — irrelevant here since the window is sub-microsecond anyway.

Net: real code, accurate field order, but the bug it predicts cannot occur. The proposed fix (publish ts LAST) is nonetheless a sound, free defensive convention — it makes the publication-ordering invariant robust against a FUTURE edit that inserts a yield point between the field writes — so I'd accept it as hardening, not as a defect fix. Severity should drop from medium to info.
- **_HsDeferredChunkTick keeps registering hotstrings while the script is paused (no A_IsSuspended guard)** (gap-hse-registry-mutation-vs-onchar-reader, medium) — The factual claims check out against source: _HsDeferredChunkTick (modules/hotstrings.ahk:997) has no A_IsSuspended guard, re-arms via SetTimer(_HsDeferredChunkTick, -_HS_DEFERRED_CHUNK_GAP_MS) at line 1025 until _HS_DeferredQueue drains, and the sibling InputHook callbacks _OnPrefixChar/_OnPrefixKeyDown DO carry the guard (lib/hotstrings/hotstring_prefix_watcher.ahk:659 and :861). But the finding misapplies the project invariant.

The project-suspend-pause-invariant is explicitly scoped in docs/PROJECT_MEMORY.md:919: "every InputHook/timer/OnMessage callback THAT PRODUCES AN OBSERVABLE SIDE EFFECT MUST early-return on A_IsSuspended." The enumerated observable effects are tooltip, LLM prediction/HTTP, keylogger recording, WPM widget, gesture action — i.e. things that activate user-visible behaviour or record data ("aucun tooltip ou autre truc ne doit s'activer"). _HsDeferredChunkTick produces NONE of these. Its body (lines 1000-1024) only calls LoadHotstringsSection -> _HsCacheRegisterSection -> CreateHotstring, which populates the custom HSE registry Maps (HSE_RegistryByLastChar, HSE_StarSpecs, etc. — see hotstrings_cache.ahk:357-388, hotstring_engine_main.ahk:253). No Send, no tooltip, no HTTP, no logging of user data.

Decisively, this is a CUSTOM engine, not native AHK Hotstring(): I confirmed there is no native Hotstring() armed in lib/hotstrings/. A trigger registered while suspended is fully INERT, because the sole live firing path is HSE_DispatchMatch, invoked only from _OnPrefixChar at hotstring_prefix_watcher.ahk:749 — and _OnPrefixChar already early-returns on A_IsSuspended at line 659 (before any HSE_FeedChar/DispatchMatch). So even mid-warm-up, a row added during pause cannot fire until the user un-pauses, at which point it should exist anyway. The finding itself concedes "When the user later un-pauses, behaviour is correct, so the leak is invisible" — under the invariant's own definition (observable side effect), there is nothing to leak. The analogy to _OnPrefixChar is wrong: that guard suppresses live keystroke REACTION (tooltips/expansion), whereas the tick does build-time DATA POPULATION behind that same gate.

Concurrency model: AHK is cooperatively single-threaded; the tick yields between ~150-row chunks (gap timer at line 1025) precisely so the keystroke InputHook runs between chunks — that already protects against the real #1 concern (dropped keys during normal typing). The "wastes CPU during pause" point is real but trivial (~3000 rows, one-time, off critical path) and not a correctness issue. The proposed fix is harmless-to-tidy but NOT required by the invariant; ironically its re-check SetTimer(-250) keeps a timer alive during pause too.
- **KL_Roi_ProcessWord / KL_Roi_OnHotstring do synchronous disk I/O (KL_AppendLog → fh.Write) inline on the keystroke thread** (gap-keylogger-roi-onchar-race-and-pause, medium) — The finding's central premise — "the hook's design intent is that NOTHING hits disk on the keystroke path... yet the ROI path puts a write right back on it" — is contradicted by the actual source. KL_AppendLog (the function whose body ends in the synchronous fh.Write at keylogger.ahk:650) is ALREADY invoked inline on the keystroke pseudo-thread by several non-ROI callers: (1) KL_Hook_OnChar (keylogger_hook.ahk:216) calls KL_Hook_RefreshContext, which on an app/title switch synchronously calls KL_FlushBuffer (line 173 → fh.Write), KL_LogAppSwitch (line 174 → fh.Write) and KL_LogWindowSwitch (line 182 → fh.Write); (2) KL_Hook_OnKeyDown (line 277) calls KL_LogShortcut → KL_AppendLog → fh.Write on every detected chord; (3) KL_LogHotstring (keylogger.ahk:782) performs TWO synchronous fh.Write calls (KL_FlushBuffer + the hotstring event) on the keystroke thread before it ever reaches KL_Roi_OnHotstring. So inline single-line disk writes on the OnChar/OnKeyDown thread are the pre-existing norm, not an ROI-introduced violation.

The cited "design intent" evidence is mischaracterized. The KL_Hook_Tick comment (keylogger_hook.ahk:346) and module header (lines 37-41) are explicitly about keeping KL_IngestOnce — the heavy SQLite batch ingest of up to INGEST_BATCH_LINES=5000 lines — off the keystroke thread, NOT a blanket guarantee that no lightweight single-line fh.Write fires inline. The header describes the typing BUFFER being RAM-batched and flushed on a timer; that same file calls KL_FlushBuffer inline in RefreshContext on app switch. The keylogger.ahk:37-52 hot-path notes describe KL_AppendLog as an accepted, optimized inline write (persistent cached FileObject handle, no per-call fh.Flush, OS write-buffering) — i.e. the architecture deliberately ACCEPTS a buffered inline fh.Write as the cost model, precisely to avoid the open/close-per-keystroke tax.

The ROI writes are also strictly RARER than the writes already on the path: the candidate write (line 192) fires only on the exact equality cnt = REPEAT_THRESHOLD (once ever per ≥4-char non-trigger word), and the snapshot write (line 139) fires 1-in-10 hotstrings. Both are negligible increments over the per-app-switch and per-chord KL_AppendLog calls already present. Under the AHK single-pseudo-thread model, KL_Roi_OnChar runs synchronously inside the OnChar fan-out with no Sleep/rest point between Keylogger.buffer_events.Push and the ROI call, so it adds no new interleaving or reentrancy hazard beyond what KL_AppendLog universally carries.

The only true residual risk — a stalled AV-locked fh.Write tripping Windows' LowLevelHooksTimeout and dropping a keystroke — is a pre-existing, architecture-wide property of EVERY KL_AppendLog call, already deliberately mitigated. Singling out the ROI module is incorrect, and the proposed fix (deferring only the two ROI calls to an in-RAM queue) would leave the far more frequent app-switch/shortcut/hotstring inline writes on the identical path, so it does not even address the stated root cause. As scoped to keylogger_trigger_roi.ahk, this is a false positive.

---

## Zones à approfondir manuellement

- **gap-hse-registry-mutation-vs-onchar-reader** — NO unit audited the HSE registration ORCHESTRATOR (modules/hotstrings.ahk) nor the engine's registry-mutation primitives against the live OnChar reader. Two concrete unserialized-mutation races on hot-path state: (1) The boot deferred chunk driver _HsDeferredChunkTick (modules/hotstrings.ahk:997) runs on SetTimer(-1) armed at boot+1500ms (ErgoptiPlus.ahk:2245), AFTER the InputHook + keylogger hooks are live, registering ~3000 emoji/symbol rows in 150-row chunks. Each chunk calls LoadHotstringsSection -> _HsCacheRegisterSection -> CreateHotstring -> HSE_Register, which does HSE_RegistryByLastChar[k].Push(Spec) AND HSE_StarSpecs.Push / HSE_StarByTriggerCI updates with NO Critical. The chunk-tick thread is interruptible mid-HSE_Register by a keystroke's _OnPrefixChar -> HSE_FeedChar -> HSE_FindMatchAtEnd, which then reads a half-updated registry (a Spec pushed into the last-char bucket but not yet into the star index, or vice-versa) -> torn read / mis-match / dropped expansion while the user types during the boot warm-up. The tick also has no A_IsSuspended guard, so it keeps registering while paused. (2) RebuildHotstringsLive (ui/tray_menu.ahk:2798) does HSE_RegistryClear() (resets every registry Map to empty) + HSE_HardReset() + RegisterAllHotstrings() synchronously with NO Critical on a menu-callback thread; the tray-menu unit flagged the unbalanced-Critical bypass dispatch but NOT that during the clear+rebuild window the OnChar reader sees a CLEARED or partially-rebuilt registry (every expansion silently no-fires mid-rebuild). HotstringPrefixWatcherRebuildIndex (also SetTimer-armed at boot and called from both paths) rebuilds the preview index OnChar reads, same class. Verify: does any Critical or generation-guard serialize HSE_Register/HSE_RegistryClear against _OnPrefixChar's HSE_FindMatchAtEnd? If not, this is the brief's #4 shared-mutable-state race on the single hottest function, plus a pause-invariant leak on the deferred tick.
  - Fichiers : `static/ergopti_plus/windows/modules/hotstrings.ahk`, `static/ergopti_plus/windows/lib/hotstrings/hotstring_engine.ahk`, `static/ergopti_plus/windows/lib/hotstrings/hotstring_engine_main.ahk`, `static/ergopti_plus/windows/lib/hotstrings/hotstrings_cache.ahk`, `static/ergopti_plus/windows/ui/tray_menu.ahk`
- **gap-keylogger-roi-onchar-race-and-pause** — keylogger_trigger_roi.ahk runs on the keystroke hot path (KL_Roi_OnChar is called from KL_Hook_OnChar on EVERY character, line ~160) yet keylogger-core focused on the buffer/flush/ingest and keylogger-sensors explicitly scoped itself as 'hot-path exposure LOW' and excluded keystroke-dropping — so this per-char module was never deeply read. Two concrete issues: (1) KL_Roi_ProcessWord mutates the KLRoi.word_counts Map on the keystroke thread (Push, then a prune loop that iterates the whole Map and Deletes entries when it exceeds MAX_TRACKED_WORDS, lines ~201-210) AND calls KL_AppendLog (a synchronous disk write per the e2e-lost-keystroke finding) on every REPEAT_THRESHOLD crossing — synchronous I/O + a full-Map prune scan on the keystroke thread, a LowLevelHooksTimeout drop risk. (2) KL_Roi_HalflifeTick (SetTimer, KL_Roi_Start line ~260) ENUMERATES KLRoi.word_counts / trigger_last_use and calls KL_AppendLog while KL_Roi_OnChar on the keystroke thread concurrently Pushes/Deletes word_counts — a torn-Map-during-enumeration cross-thread race (same class as the app_categories race keylogger-sensors flagged, but a DIFFERENT map nobody examined) that can throw 'modified during enumeration'. Neither KL_Roi_OnChar/ProcessWord nor the half-life tick has its own A_IsSuspended guard, so the tick keeps writing PII candidate/half-life events while paused (the e2e-suspend-pause critical), and KL_AppendLog itself lacks the guard.
  - Fichiers : `static/ergopti_plus/windows/modules/keylogger/keylogger_trigger_roi.ahk`, `static/ergopti_plus/windows/modules/keylogger/keylogger_hook.ahk`, `static/ergopti_plus/windows/modules/keylogger/keylogger.ahk`
- **gap-hotstrings-cache-toml-fallback-parse-divergence** — hotstrings_cache.ahk is the top-of-history commit (self-healing .tsv cache replacing ~1MB generated AHK) and is on the boot registration path, but no unit deeply read it. Worth a fresh look at correctness/robustness edges nobody owns: (1) _HotstringsCacheBuildRows re-implements the LoadHotstringsSection TOML scan with _HOTSTRING_ENTRY_PATTERN and claims to reproduce it 1:1 — verify the cache-built rows and the runtime TOML fallback cannot diverge on the same TOML (a divergence silently registers DIFFERENT hotstrings on a fast boot vs a rebuilt boot). (2) Freshness: _HotstringsCacheIsFresh uses FileGetTime 'M' minute-granularity; a TOML edited in the same minute as the .tsv write would be judged fresh and the edit silently ignored until the next boot. (3) HS_CACHE_MARKER literal star substituted at register time — confirm a user MagicKey containing a backslash or tab cannot corrupt a row after StrReplace. (4) _HsCacheRegisterSection is the chunked registrar feeding the deferred race in gap-1; confirm StartIdx/MaxCount slicing can never double-register or skip a row across chunks when _HS_CACHE_ROWS is the live map.
  - Fichiers : `static/ergopti_plus/windows/lib/hotstrings/hotstrings_cache.ahk`, `static/ergopti_plus/windows/lib/toml/toml_loader.ahk`

> Tout finding dont la faisabilité de test est `needs-live-hardware` doit être validé en frappe réelle sur la machine après correctif.
